Closed G 2 \mathrm{G}_{2} structures on compact manifolds satisfying the known topological obstructions to holonomy G 2 \mathrm{G}_{2} metrics
Lucía Martín-Merchán, Humboldt-Universität zu Berlin

Abstract
We construct new compact manifolds endowed with closed G 2 \mathrm{G}_{2} structures that satisfy the topological properties found by Joyce and Baraglia for the existence of a torsion-free G 2 \mathrm{G}_{2} structure in the same cohomology class. Those manifolds arise as resolutions of orbifolds of the form M / ℤ 2 M/\mathbb{Z}_{2} , where M M itself does not admit any torsion-free G 2 \mathrm{G}_{2} structure. We develop an equivariant resolution procedure for closed G 2 \mathrm{G}_{2} orbifolds with ℤ 2 \mathbb{Z}_{2} isotropy, and analyze some topological properties of the resolved manifolds, including their first Pontryagin class and certain triple Massey products.



1
Introduction

A
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure on a
7
-dimensional manifold
M
is a reduction of the structure group of its frame bundle from
\mathrm{Gl}(7,\mathbb{R})
to the exceptional Lie group
\mathrm{G}_{2}
. Such a
reduction exists if and only if
M
is orientable and spin. A
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure is
determined by
\varphi\in\Omega^{3}(M)
with pointwise stabilizer equal to
\mathrm{G}_{2}
, and it induces a Riemannian metric
g
and an orientation. The structure is said to be torsion-free when
\nabla\varphi=0
, or equivalently, when
d\varphi=0
and
d(\star\varphi)=0

[
17
]
. In this case,
g
has holonomy contained in
\mathrm{G}_{2}
, and it is Ricci flat.

Metrics with holonomy
\mathrm{G}_{2}
were predicted in Berger’s classification
[
5
]
, but their construction in the compact setting presents analytical difficulties.
The first examples were obtained by Joyce via resolution of flat orbifolds
[
21
,
22
]
. Later, Kovalev
[
26
]
introduced the twisted connected sum method, and in joint work with Lee
[
27
]
, produced more examples. This method was later extended by Corti, Haskins, Nordström, and Pacini
[
10
]
, and further refined by Nordström
[
32
]
. The most recent method, by Joyce and Karigiannis
[
25
]
consits on resolving torsion-free
\mathrm{G}_{2}
orbifolds with isolated singularities of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
isotropy.
Additional examples using it appear in
[
36
]
.

The known topological constraints imposed by the existence of a holonomy
\mathrm{G}_{2}
metric
on a compact manifold seem to be mild. These include two results from Joyce’s foundational work
[
22
, Proposition 1.1.1, Lemma 1.1.2]
, and a further condition established by Baraglia
[
3
, Proposition 3.1]
. Let
M
be a compact
7
-dimensional manifold that
admits a holonomy
\mathrm{G}_{2}
metric whose associated
3
-form lies in the cohomology class
[\phi]\in H^{3}(M)
then:

P1)

\pi_{1}(M)
is finite.

P2)

If the orientation of
M
is induced by
\phi
then,

\int_{M}{p_{1}(M)\wedge\phi}<0,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\int_{M}{\alpha\wedge\alpha\wedge\phi}<0\quad\mbox{ for all }[\alpha]\in H^{2}(M),\,[\alpha]\neq 0.

(1)

P3)

There is no fibration
\pi\colon M\to B
onto a
3
-dimensional base that is locally trivial.

For some time, it was conjectured that compact holonomy
\mathrm{G}_{2}
manifolds; the same was expected for compact manifolds with special holonomy. For simply connected manifolds, formality implies that the rational homotopy type is determined by the rational cohomology algebra; in particular, the rational homotopy groups can be computed from it.
This expectation was motivated by the work of Deligne, Griffiths, Morgan, and Sullivan
[
12
]
, who proved that compact Kähler manifolds are formal, and further supported by
the work of Amann and Kapovitch
[
1
]
, which showed formality of compact quaternion Kähler manifolds with positive scalar curvature. In the
\mathrm{G}_{2}
case, partial results included the formality of compact torsion-free
\mathrm{G}_{2}
manifolds with
b_{1}=0
and
b_{2}<4

[
11
]
, with
b_{1}>0

[
18
]
, and an example constructed by Joyce
[
2
]
. The conjecture for compact holonomy
\mathrm{G}_{2}
manifolds was finally disproved by the author in
[
30
]
.

Closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structures, i.e., those satisfying
d\varphi=0
, play an important role in constructing compact holonomy
\mathrm{G}_{2}
manifolds. All known methods, except for that of
[
25
]
,
begin with a smooth manifold obtained by gluing two open pieces, each equipped with a torsion-free
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure and
with matching asymptotic behaviour.
Interpolating these forms yields a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure whose torsion
\star d(\star\varphi)
is small in a precise sense. The construction in
[
25
]
includes a piece that does not come equipped with torsion-free
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structures, so additional corrections are needed to obtain a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure with small torsion.
In any case, Joyce’s foundational results
[
21
]
guarantee that such structure can be deformed into a torsion-free one.

Prior to this paper, all known simply connected manifolds with a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure were those that admit a holonomy
\mathrm{G}_{2}
metric. In addition,
it remains an open problem whether there exist simply connected manifolds with closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structures that do not admit torsion-free ones. For compact manifolds with
b_{1}>0
,
the existence of a torsion-free
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure implies stronger topological properties that are not satisfied by some known manifolds admitting closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structures. A classification of nilmanifolds with this property is provided in
[
9
]
, and further examples with
b_{1}=1
were constructed in
[
15
,
29
]
by finding involutions on two of such nilmanifolds and resolving the singularities of the resulting quotient. Solvmanifolds admitting closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structures appear in
[
14
,
28
]
.
Another open problem is to identify conditions, beyond small torsion, under which one can deform a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure to reduce the torsion and potentially produce a torsion-free one. While the problem currently seems out of reach, geometric flows have been proposed as tools in this direction. In particular, the Laplacian flow introduced by Bryant
[
8
]
, evolves a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure within its cohomology class in the direction of its Hodge Laplacian. Motivated by these questions, we prove:

Theorem 1
.

There are compact manifolds
\widetilde{X}

endowed with a one-parameter family of closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structures,
\widetilde{\varphi}_{t},
such that the properties
(P1)
,
(P2)
,
(P3)
hold for each pair
(\widetilde{X},\tilde{\varphi}_{t})
. These are constructed by resolving the singularities of an orbifold
M/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, where
M
does not carry any torsion-free
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure.

We do not rule out the existence of holonomy
\mathrm{G}_{2}
metrics on them. However,
\widetilde{\varphi}_{t}
does not have small torsion, as it is constructed from a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure on
M
, which itself admits no closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure satisfying the hypotheses of Joyce’s theorem.
Furthermore, we verify that, with the exception of one example, their topological properties distinguish them from all known compact holonomy
\mathrm{G}_{2}
manifolds constructed to date. This suggests that a deeper study of these examples could provide new insights, potentially leading to obstructions to the existence of torsion-free
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structures or providing a testing ground for existing methods, such as geometric flows. We summarize the studied topological properties in a table:

Name

Fundamental group

(b_{2},b_{3})

Formal

\widetilde{X}_{1,3}^{1}

\mathbb{Z}_{3}

(3,11)

Yes

\widetilde{X}_{1,6}^{1}

\{1\}

(2,10)

Yes

\widetilde{X}_{2,4}^{1}

\mathbb{Z}_{2}

(4,17)

Yes

\widetilde{X}_{3,4}^{1}

\{1\}

(8,29)

Yes

\widetilde{Z}_{2,4}

\mathbb{Z}_{2}

(4,9)

No

\widetilde{Z}_{3,4}

\{1\}

(8,13)

No

\widetilde{X}_{1,3}^{2}

\{1\}

(2,25)

Yes

\widetilde{X}_{1,6}^{2}

\{1\}

(2,25)

Yes

\widetilde{X}_{2,4}^{2}

\{1\}

(7,46)

Yes

In our construction, the initial manifolds are non-formal and have
b_{1}=1
. Among these, all but three arise as mapping tori of diffeomorphisms of
T^{6}
and they are finitely covered by nilmanifolds whose universal cover is the Lie group
G
with
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
spanned by a basis
(e^{1},\dots,e^{7})
such that
(de^{1},\dots,de^{7})=(0,0,0,e^{12},e^{13},0,0)
. The remaining are mapping tori of diffeomorphisms on the product of a
K3
surface and
T^{2}
. These are finitely covered by the resolution of the quotient of nilmanifolds with universal cover
G
under a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action.
The closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structures we use come from a left-invariant closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure on
G
, found in
[
9
]
. The Laplacian flow for this
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure was studied in
[
16
, Theorem 4.2]
. The solution exists for
t\in(-\frac{3}{10},\infty)
, and the associated metrics are nilsolitons. The curvature of such metrics tends to
0
as
t\to\infty
.

Our examples are constructed using the resolution method developed in
[
29
]
, which is analogous to the approach in
[
25
]
, and consists on blowing-up the normal bundle of the singular locus. This is a
3
-dimensional submanifold calibrated by
\varphi
. Hence, the exceptional divisor is a
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
bundle over this locus. In some cases, we require an equivariant version of this method, which is established in this paper:

Theorem 2
.

Let
(M,\varphi,g)
be a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure on a compact manifold. Suppose that
\iota\colon M\to M
is an involution such that
\iota^{*}\varphi=\varphi
and consider the orbifold
X=M/\iota
.
Let
L=\operatorname{Fix}(\iota)
be the singular locus of
X
and suppose that there is a nowhere-vanishing closed
1
-form
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(L)
.

Let
\kappa
be a finite order diffeomorphism such that
\kappa^{*}\varphi=\varphi
,
\kappa\circ\iota=\iota\circ\kappa
and
\kappa^{*}\theta=\sigma\theta
, where
\sigma\colon L\to\{\pm 1\}
is a continuous function.
Then, there is a one-parameter family of closed

\mathrm{G}_{2}
resolutions
\rho\colon(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\varphi}_{t},\widetilde{g})\to(X,\varphi,g)
,
and a finite order diffeomorphism
\tilde{\kappa}\colon\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{X}
such that
\tilde{\kappa}\circ\rho=\rho\circ\kappa
and

\tilde{\kappa}^{*}(\widetilde{\varphi}_{t})=\widetilde{\varphi}_{t}
.

The topological study of closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
resolutions began in
[
29
]
, where we computed the cohomology algebra of such spaces. In this paper, we go further by computing the first Pontryagin class and analyzing formality. To describe the results, we let
L=\sqcup_{j}L_{j}
where
L_{j}
is connected, and
Q_{j}=\rho^{-1}(L_{j})
.
For the first Pontryagin class of
\widetilde{X}
, we prove:

p_{1}(\widetilde{X})=\rho^{*}(p_{1}(M))+\sum_{j}{-3\,\rho^{*}(\mathrm{Th}[L_{j}])+2\,c_{1}(\ker(\theta|_{L_{j}}))\wedge\mathrm{Th}[Q_{j}]},

where
\mathrm{Th}[Q_{j}]
denotes the Thom class of
Q_{j}
. The complex structure on
\ker(\theta|_{L_{j}})
is determined by
\frac{1}{\|\theta\|}i(\theta^{\sharp})\varphi|_{L_{j}}
, and the
class
c_{1}(\ker(\theta|_{L_{j}}))
is computed for a specific choice of
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(L)
.
As for formality, we study triple Massey products, which are higher-order cohomological operations whose non-vanishing provides an obstruction to formality. These are partially defined: a triple Massey product
\langle[\alpha],[\beta],[\gamma]\rangle
is only defined when
[\alpha\wedge\beta]=[\beta\wedge\gamma]=0
. When this condition holds, the result is a cohomology class defined modulo an indeterminacy ideal. In
[
30
]
, such a non-vanishing triple Massey product was found in a torsion-free
\mathrm{G}_{2}
resolution, and in this paper, we analyze the mechanism behind this phenomenon, which depends on the configuration of the singular locus. More precisely, we prove:

Proposition 3
.

Let
L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4}
be different connected components of
L
diffeomorphic to a mapping torus with fiber
T^{2}
, and such that
L_{1}\sqcup-L_{2}
and
L_{3}\sqcup-L_{4}
are nullhomologous. If their
linking number is non-zero,
then the Triple Massey product

\langle\mathrm{Th}[Q_{1}]+\mathrm{Th}[Q_{2}],\mathrm{Th}[Q_{3}]+\mathrm{Th}[Q_{4}],\mathrm{Th}[Q_{3}]-\mathrm{Th}[Q_{4}]\rangle

does not vanish.

We also provide a self-contained discussion on intersection theory, which facilitates the computation of linking numbers. Combining these results, we show that the manifolds
\widetilde{Z}_{2,4}
and
\widetilde{Z}_{3,4}
are not formal. Furthermore, we show that the remaining examples are formal using the Bianchi-Massey tensor introduced in
[
11
]
.
This tensor refines triple Massey products and for
7
-manifolds with
b_{1}=0
, its vanishing is equivalent to formality. While explicit computations of the Bianchi-Massey tensor in geometric contexts are rare, our work illustrates that it can be effectively used in the setting of
\mathrm{G}_{2}
resolutions.

This paper is organized as follows. In section
2
, we provide the necessary preliminaries on mapping tori, formality of
7
-dimensional manifolds, intersection theory and resolutions of closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structures. Later, in section
3
, we study triple Massey products on resolutions and prove the formula for the first Pontryagin class. Theorem
2
for equivariant resolutions is proved in section
4
.
Finally, the examples are provided in section
5
, where we perform a detailed analysis of their topological properties and prove Theorem
1
.

Acknoweledgements
: This project started with a question of Giovanni Bazzoni and Vicente Muñoz. I thank Spiro Karigiannis and Fabian Lehmann for feedback on some sections of this manuscript.

2
Preliminaries

The present paper extends the results of
[
29
]
, making use of several techniques introduced therein. For the relevant background on orbifolds,
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structures, and rational homotopy theory,
we refer the reader to
[
29
, Section 2]
.
Here, we focus primarily on results and methods not used in that work.
In section
2.1
, we review the topological properties of mapping tori. We then introduce the Bianchi-Massey tensor in section
2.2
, and in section
2.3
, we establish some results in intersection theory relevant to the computation of triple Massey products and the Bianchi-Massey tensor. Finally, in section
2.4
, we fix notation related to
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structures and review the results from
[
29
]
used in the remaining sections.

2.1
Mapping tori

Let
N
be a smooth manifold, the mapping torus of a diffeomorphism
F\colon N\to N
is

N_{F}=\dfrac{\mathbb{R}\times N}{\{(t,p)\sim(t+m,F^{m}(p))\colon m\in\mathbb{Z}\}}=\dfrac{[0,1]\times N}{(0,p)\sim(1,F(p))}.

(2)

We denote
q\colon[0,1]\times N\to N_{F}
the quotient projection; we also write
[t,p]=q(t,p)
. If
N
is oriented
and
f\colon N\to N
is an orientation preserving diffeomorphism, then
N_{F}
is oriented and its orientation is induced by the product orientation on
\mathbb{R}\times N
.
In section
5.1
, we define objects in
N_{F}
from those in
N
, more precisely:

1.

Let
\alpha_{t}\in\Omega^{k-1}(N)
,
\beta_{t}\in\Omega^{k}(N)
a smooth path of forms with
t\in[0,1]
, then
\gamma=dt\wedge\alpha_{t}+\beta_{t}
induces a
k
-form on
N_{F}
if

F^{*}\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{0}
, and
F^{*}\beta_{1}=\beta_{0}.

2.

Let
\xi,\eta\colon N\to N
be diffeomorphisms, then
\iota[t,p]=[t,\xi(p)]
is well-defined when

\xi\circ F=F\circ\xi
. In addition,

\kappa[t,p]=[1-t,\eta(p)]
is well-defined when

\eta\circ F=F^{-1}\circ\eta.

Their fixed point sets are:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Fix}(\iota)=

\displaystyle q([0,1]\times\operatorname{Fix}(\xi)),

(3)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Fix}(\kappa)=

\displaystyle q(\{0\}\times\operatorname{Fix}(\eta\circ F)\cup\{1/2\}\times\operatorname{Fix}(\eta)).

(4)

3.

Assume that
\gamma=dt\wedge\alpha_{t}+\beta_{t}\in\Omega^{k}(N_{F})
, and
\iota,\kappa\colon N_{F}\to N_{F}
are well-defined. Then
\iota^{*}\gamma=\gamma
when

\xi^{*}\alpha_{t}=\alpha_{t}
and
\xi^{*}\beta_{t}=\beta_{t}
, and

\kappa^{*}\gamma=\gamma

if
\eta^{*}\alpha_{t}=-\alpha_{1-t}
and
\eta^{*}\beta_{t}=\beta_{1-t}
.

Note that
N_{F}
is diffeomorphic to
N_{F^{-1}}
. We now
review some topological properties of mapping tori.
It is well-known that

\pi_{1}(N_{F})\cong\pi_{1}(N)\rtimes_{F_{*}}\mathbb{Z},

(5)

where
F_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(N)\to\pi_{1}(N)
is the map induced by
F
. Here, we choose the basepoint
[1/2,x_{0}]
, where

x_{0}\in N
is a fixed point of
F
.
We obtain a set of generators by identifying
N
with the level set
\{\frac{1}{2}\}\times N
, and considering the loop

{\gamma}_{0}\colon[0,1]\to N_{F},\qquad{\gamma}_{0}(s)=[{1}/{2}-s,x_{0}],

(6)

whose homotopy class spans the
\mathbb{Z}
factor in (
5
).
As usual, we denote
\gamma^{-1}(s)=\gamma(1-s)
. The product relation
[\rho]\sim[\gamma_{0}]\cdot F_{*}[\rho]\cdot[\gamma_{0}]^{-1}
for
[\rho]\in\pi_{1}(N)
can be deduced as follows. Denote
\tilde{\rho}(s)=[1/2,\rho(s)]
, and
\widetilde{F\circ\rho}(s)=[-1/2,\rho(s)]=[1/2,(F\circ\rho)(s)]
. A homotopy
H_{r}
between
\tilde{\rho}
and
\gamma_{0}\cdot\widetilde{F\circ\rho}\cdot\gamma_{0}^{-1}
is constructed as a sequence of three paths. First travel in the
t
-direction from
[1,2,[x_{0}]]
to
[1/2-r,[x_{0}]]
. Then move horizontally along
s\mapsto[1/2-r,\rho(s)]
and finally reverse the first path to return.

Some cohomological properties of mapping tori are contained in
[
4
]
. The Mayer–Vietoris sequence, applied choosing the open cover
U_{1}=q((\frac{1}{5},\frac{4}{5})\times N)
,
U_{2}=q([0,\frac{1}{3})\times N\cup(\frac{2}{3},1]\times N)
, yields:

Lemma 4
.

[
4
, Lemma 12]

Let
N
be a smooth manifold and let
F\colon N\to N
be a diffeomorphism. Then, there is a split short exact sequence

0\to C^{m-1}\to H^{m}(N_{F})\to K^{m}\to 0,

(7)

where
K^{m}
and
C^{m}
are the kernel and cokernel of the map
(F^{*}-\mathrm{Id})\colon H^{m}(N)\to H^{m}(N)
.

The inclusion
\delta^{*}\colon C^{m-1}\to H^{m}(N_{F})
is determined by the connecting isomorphism of the sequence. Given an increasing bump function

\rho\colon[0,1]\to[0,1]
which equals
0
on
t\leq\frac{1}{3}
and
1
on
t\geq\frac{2}{3}
, we can write

\delta^{*}[\beta]=[d\rho\wedge\beta]
. In particular,
\delta^{*}(1)=[dt]
. The surjection
H^{m}(N_{F})\to K^{m}
is
the restriction to
q(\{\frac{1}{4}\}\times N)
. To find a section, we pick
[\alpha]\in K^{m}
, and
\beta\in\Omega^{m-1}(N)
with
d\beta=\alpha-F^{*}\alpha
, the form

\widetilde{\alpha}=F^{*}\alpha+d(\rho\beta),

(8)

is a well-defined closed form on
N_{F}
with
[\widetilde{\alpha}]|_{t=1/4}=F^{*}[\alpha]=[\alpha]
.

In section
5.3
we need to find the cohomology classes that remain invariant under the action of
\iota,\kappa\colon N_{F}\to N_{F}
. From the discussion above, we observe that the action preserves the subspaces
C^{m-1}
and
K^{m}
. For
[\beta]\in C^{m-1}
, we have
\iota^{*}\delta^{*}[\beta]=\delta^{*}[\beta]
when
\xi^{*}[\beta]\equiv[\beta]\mod\mathrm{Im}(F^{*}-\mathrm{Id})
and
\kappa^{*}\delta^{*}[\beta]=\delta^{*}[\beta]
if
\eta^{*}[\beta]\equiv-[\beta]\mod\mathrm{Im}(F^{*}-\mathrm{Id})
. Moreover, for forms in
K^{m}
,

\iota
-invariance (respectively,
\kappa
-invariance) is equivalent to
\xi
-invariance (respectively,
\eta
-invariant).

Finally, the formality of the mapping torus
N_{F}
depends on the multiplicity of
1
as an eigenvalue of
F^{*}
. In
[
4
]
,
the multiplicity of an eigenvalue
\lambda
of an endomorphism
A\colon V\to V
is defined by its multiplicity as a root of the minimal polynomial of
A
.

Theorem 5
.

[
4
, Corollary 16]

Assume that there is some
m\geq 2
such that
K^{n}=\{0\}
for
n\leq m-1
and that
F^{*}\colon H^{m}(N)\to H^{m}(N)
has the eigenvalue
\lambda=1
with multiplicity
r\geq 2
. Then,
N_{F}
is not formal.

2.2
Formality of 7-dimensional manifolds with
b_{1}=0

The Bianchi-Massey tensor, introduced in
[
11
]
, determines whether a
7
-dimensional manifold with
b_{1}=0
is formal. In addition, for those that are simply connected, the Bianchi-Massey tensor and the cohomology algebra encode the rational homotopy type. In section
5.3.4
we use this tensor to show that some of the manifolds constructed in section
5.2
are formal. To establish the non-formality of the others, we find a non-vanishing triple Massey product on them. Below, we briefly recall the notions and results needed. We remark that,
while
[
11
]
works over
\mathbb{Q}
, we work over
\mathbb{R}
because our focus is on formality, which descends from
\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{Q}
(see
[
35
, Theorem 12.1]
).

Triple Massey products encode linking phenomena between submanifolds and provide obstructions to formality. Given cohomology classes
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in H^{*}(M)
, their triple Massey product
\langle\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\rangle
is defined when

\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}=0
and
\xi_{2}\wedge\xi_{3}=0
. In this case,
we choose
representatives
\alpha_{i}\in\Omega^{*}(M)
of
\xi_{i}
, and primitives
\beta_{12}
and
\beta_{23}
of
\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}
and
\alpha_{2}\wedge\alpha_{3}
. Define the cohomology class

y=[\beta_{12}\wedge\alpha_{3}-(-1)^{\deg(\alpha_{1})}\alpha_{1}\wedge\beta_{23}],

and let
\pi\colon H^{*}(M)\to H^{*}(M)/\langle\xi_{1},\xi_{3}\rangle H^{*}(M)
be the quotient projection. Then, one can check that
\pi(y)
does not depend on the chosen representatives. We define
\langle\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\rangle=\pi(y)
, and we say it vanishes if

y\in\langle\xi_{1},\xi_{3}\rangle H^{*}(M)
.

Lemma 6
.

[
13
, Proposition 2.90]
Triple Massey products vanish on formal manifolds.

We now adapt the definition provided in
[
11
]
of the Bianchi-Masey tensor to the particular set-up of a
7
-dimensional manifold
M
with
b_{1}=0
. Let
\mathcal{Z}^{*}(M)
be the subspace of closed forms, and pick a linear map
\alpha\colon H^{2}(M)\to\mathcal{Z}^{2}(M)
that chooses a representative of each cohomology class.
Consider the wedge product map

\wedge\colon\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(H^{2}(M))\to H^{4}(M),

and denote
E^{4}(M)=\ker(\wedge)
.
Choose a homomorphism
\gamma\colon E^{4}(M)\to\Omega^{3}(M)
such that
d\gamma(e)=\alpha^{2}(e)
for every
e\in E^{4}(M)
. Of course, we understand
\alpha^{2}(\xi_{1}\odot\xi_{2})=\alpha(\xi_{1})\wedge\alpha(\xi_{2})
. The graded symmetrization of

E^{4}(M)\otimes E^{4}(M)\to H^{7}(M),\qquad e\otimes e\mapsto[\gamma(e)\wedge\alpha^{2}(e^{\prime})],

is the extension of
e\odot e^{\prime}\mapsto[\gamma(e)\wedge\alpha^{2}(e^{\prime})]
because

d(\gamma(e)\wedge\gamma(e^{\prime}))=\alpha^{2}(e)\wedge\gamma(e^{\prime})-\gamma(e)\wedge\alpha^{2}(e^{\prime})
. Consider the full symmetrization tensor

\mathfrak{S}\colon\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(H^{2}(M))\to\mathrm{Sym}^{4}(H^{2}(M)),\qquad(\xi_{1}\odot\xi_{2})\odot(\xi_{3}\odot\xi_{4})\longmapsto\xi_{1}\cdot\xi_{2}\cdot\xi_{3}\cdot\xi_{4},

and define
\mathcal{B}^{8}(M)=\ker(\mathfrak{S}|_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(E^{4}(M))})
. The Bianchi Massey tensor of
M
is

\mathcal{F}_{M}\colon\mathcal{B}^{8}(M)\to H^{7}(M),\quad\mathcal{F}_{M}(e\odot e^{\prime})=[\gamma(e)\wedge(\alpha^{2})(e^{\prime})],

which turns out to be independent of the choices for
\alpha
and
\gamma
. The following results are relevant for us:

Proposition 7
.

[
11
, Corollary 3.10, Theorem 1.14]

Let
M
be an oriented
7
-dimensional manifold with
b_{1}=0
, then

1.

M
is formal if and only if
\mathcal{F}_{M}
vanishes.

2.

If
b_{2}\leq 3
and there is
\xi\in H^{3}(M)
such that
(\cdot)\wedge\xi\colon H^{2}(M)\to H^{5}(M)
is an isomorphism, then
M
is formal.

Remark 1
.

[
11
, Theorem 1.14]
is stated for simply-connected
7
-manifolds but it just needs
b_{1}=0
. This result relies on
[
11
, Corollary 3.10]
together with

[
11
, Proposition 5.1]
, which shows
\mathcal{B}^{8}(M)=0
under these assumptions.
However, the framework for rational homotopy theory via minimal models only applies to nilpotent spaces, namely spaces
M
for which
\pi_{1}(M)
is a nilpotent group, and the action of
\pi_{1}(M)
on
\pi_{n}(M)
is nilpotent.

2.3
Intersection theory

The Thom class of a compact oriented submanifold:

Let
L
be a compact manifold (maybe with boundary) and
E\to L
an oriented oriented vector bundle with rank
r
. A compactly supported closed form
\upsilon\in\Omega_{c}^{r}(E)
is a Thom form of
E
if
it integrates to
1
on each fiber.
The Thom isomorphism theorem states
[
6
, Theorem 6.17]
that integration along the fibers yield an isomorphism
H_{c}^{i+r}(E)\to H^{i}(L)
. Hence, all Thom forms determine the same cohomology class, called the Thom class, which generates
H_{c}^{r}(E)
. In addition, we can assume that the support of a Thom form is arbitrarily close to the zero section (see
[
6
, Remark 12.4.1]
). A property that we will use later is that the Thom class of the direct sum of two vector bundles is the wedge product of their Thom classes (
[
6
, Proposition 6.19]
).

Let
M
be a closed oriented manifold and let
L
be a compact submanifold of codimension
r
that may have boundary. Fixed some metric on
M
, we consider the normal bundle
\nu(L)
of
L
, oriented so that the splitting
TM|_{L}=TL\oplus\nu(L)
preserves orientations. Let
U
be a tubular neighborhood of
L
, and
\upsilon
a Thom form of
\nu(L)
supported on
\exp^{-1}(U)
. We say that
\tau=(\exp_{L})_{*}(\upsilon)\in\Omega^{r}_{c}(U)
is a Thom form for
L
. Of course,
\tau
yields to a generator of
H^{r}_{c}(U)
, characterized by the property that it integrates to

1
over the fibers of the nearest point projection
U\to L
.
If
\partial L=\emptyset
, then there is a natural inclusion
(\Omega_{c}^{*}(U),d)\hookrightarrow(\Omega^{*}(M),d)
, and we denote by
\mathrm{Th}[L]\in H^{r}(M)
the cohomology class determined by
\tau
. It turns out that
\mathrm{Th}[L]
is the Poincaré dual of
L
(see
[
6
, Proposition 6.24]
).
Otherwise,
\tau
does not extend to
M
, as it is non-zero on a disk bundle over
\partial L
.

Remark 2
.

Let
\iota\colon M\to M
be an orientation-preserving involution with fixed points, and let
g
be a
\iota
-invariant metric. Suppose
L\subset\operatorname{Fix}(\iota)
is an oriented connected component with (even) codimension
r
; then
\iota^{*}
acts on
\nu(L)
as
-\mathrm{Id}
.
The normal bundle of
L
on the orbifold
X=M/\iota
is
\nu(L)/\iota_{*}
. By the tubular neighborhood theorem for orbifolds, the exponential map yields a diffeomorphism from a neighborhood of

L
on
\nu(L)/\iota_{*}
and
U=V/\iota
, for some
\iota
-invariant tubular neighborhood
V\subset M
of
L
. Similarly to
[
29
, Section 2]
, we have
(\Omega_{c}(U),d)=(\Omega_{c}(V)^{\iota_{*}},d)
, and an average argument yields
H^{*}_{c}(U)\cong H^{*}_{c}(V)^{\iota}
. Since
\iota_{*}
is an orientation-preserving isomorphism of
\nu(L)
, the pullback
\iota^{*}\upsilon
of a Thom form
\upsilon
of
L
on
M
is again a Thom form. Thus
\frac{1}{2}(\upsilon+\iota^{*}\upsilon)
is a
\iota
-invariant Thom form, and
H^{r}_{c}(U)\cong\mathbb{R}
with the isomorphism given by integration along the fibers.

We say that
\tau\in\Omega_{c}(U)
is a Thom form of
L
if it integrates to
1
on the fibers of the normal bundle
\nu(L)/\iota_{*}
. Note that,
\tau\in\Omega_{c}^{r}(U)
is a Thom form of
L
on
X
if and only if
\frac{1}{2}\tau\in\Omega_{c}^{r}(V)
is a
\iota
-invariant Thom form of
L
on
M
. This follows from

\int_{\nu_{x}(L)/\iota_{*}}{\exp^{*}_{L}(\tau)}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\nu_{x}(L)}\exp^{*}_{L}(\tau).

We also denote by
\mathrm{Th}[L]\in H^{r}(X)
the class of a Thom form
\tau
of
L
.
This coincides with the Poincaré Dual of
L
in
X
(see
[
34
]
for a proof of Poincaré Duality for orbifolds) because for any
\alpha\in\Omega^{\dim(M)-r}(X)
we have

\int_{X}\alpha\wedge\tau=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\alpha\wedge\tau=\int_{L}\alpha,

where we used
\frac{1}{2}[\tau]=PD[L]
on
M
.

A direct consequence of
[
6
, Proposition 6.19]
is that, if
\tau_{j}\in\Omega(M_{j})
, is a Thom form of a closed oriented submanifold
L_{j}\subset M_{j}
(for
j=1,2
), then
a Thom form of
L_{1}\times L_{2}
, is given by
(-1)^{\mathrm{codim}(L_{1})\dim(L_{2})}\tau_{1}\wedge\tau_{2}
.

Intersections:
Consider two compact oriented submanifolds
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, with
\partial L_{2}=\emptyset
. If they intersect transversally outside
\partial L_{1}
, then
L_{1}\cap L_{2}
is a closed submanifold (see
[
20
]
, p. 60). Transversality implies
\nu(L_{1}\cap L_{2})\cong\nu(L_{1})|_{L_{1}\cap L_{2}}\oplus\nu(L_{2})|_{L_{1}\cap L_{2}}
. Hence, we provide
\nu(L_{1}\cap L_{2})
with the direct sum orientation and then orient
T(L_{1}\cap L_{2})
so that
TM|_{L_{1}\cap L_{2}}=T(L_{1}\cap L_{2})\oplus\nu(L_{1}\cap L_{2})
as oriented vector bundles. If
\tau_{1}
is a Thom form for
L_{1}
on a tubular neighborhood of
L_{1}
(which might not extend to
M
) and
\tau_{2}
is a Thom form of
L_{2}
, then
\tau_{1}\wedge\tau_{2}
is well-defined on
M
and
[
6
, Proposition 6.19]
implies that
[\tau_{1}\wedge\tau_{2}]=\mathrm{Th}[L_{1}\cap L_{2}]
.

Nullcobordant submanifolds:
If a closed oriented submanifold
L
is nullhomologous, then
\mathrm{Th}[L]=PD[L]=0
. In addition, if
L=\partial C
, Lemma
8
shows that we can use the Thom form of
C
to construct a primitive of a Thom form of
L
. Recall that
given a
(k+1)
-dimensional compact oriented manifold with boundary
C
, we endow
\partial C
with the orientation determined by the normal vector field pointing inwards
V_{i}
. That is,
(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})
is a positive basis of
T\partial C
if and only if
(v_{1},\dots,v_{k},V_{i})
is a positive basis of
TC
. If
k=3
, this is the same as saying that
(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})
is positive if
(V_{o},v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})
is positive; where
V_{o}=-V_{i}
is the normal vector pointing outwards.

Lemma 8
.

Let
M
be a smooth oriented manifold. Let
C
be
a compact oriented submanifold with boundary, and let
\tau_{\partial C}
be a Thom form of
\partial C
supported on a tubular neighborhood
V
of
\partial C
. Given a tubular neighborhood
U
of
C
, there is a form
\beta
supported on
U\cup V
such that
\beta|_{U-V}
is a Thom form of
C-V
and
d\beta=\tau_{\partial C}
.

In addition, if
\tau^{\prime}
is a Thom form of a submanifold
N
intersecting
C
transversally outside of
V
, then
\beta\wedge\tau^{\prime}
is a Thom form of
C\cap N
.

Proof.

We define the subsets
W_{s}^{t}
by:

D_{s}=\exp_{\partial C}(\{\lambda V_{i},\,\lambda\in[0,s]\}),\qquad W_{s}^{t}=\exp_{C}(\{v_{p}\in\nu(C),\,p\in D_{s},\|v_{p}\|<t\}).

Shrinking
U
, and choosing
\varepsilon,\delta>0
small enough we assume that
U=\exp_{C}(\{v_{p}\in\nu(C),\,\|v_{p}\|<\delta\})
and
W_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}\subset V
. We now claim that a Thom form of the oriented vector bundle
\langle V_{i}\rangle|_{\partial C}\to\partial C
is
\exp_{\partial C}^{*}(db)
, where
b
is a bump function defined in
U
, that satisfies
b=0
on
W_{\varepsilon/4}^{\delta}
, and
b=1
on
U-W_{\varepsilon/2}^{\delta}
. To verify this claim we observe that if
p\in\partial C
, then

\int_{\mathbb{R}V_{i}(p)}{\exp_{\partial C}^{*}d{b}}={b}(\exp_{p}((\varepsilon/2)V_{i}(p))-{b}(\exp_{p}(({\varepsilon}/{4})V_{i}(p))=1.

Given
\tau_{C}\in\Omega_{c}^{n-k}(U)
a Thom form of
C
, then
db\wedge\tau_{C}
extends to
M
as a Thom form of
\partial C
supported on
V
. The last claim follows from
[
6
, Proposition 6.19]
and the splitting
TC|_{\partial C}=T\partial C\oplus\langle V_{i}\rangle
as oriented vector bundles, which implies
\nu(\partial C)=\langle V_{i}\rangle\oplus\nu(C)|_{\partial C}
.
Indeed,
\beta^{\prime}={b}\tau_{C}
extends to a well-defined form on
M

because
b
vanishes on
W_{\varepsilon/4}^{\delta}
. In addition,
d\beta^{\prime}=db\wedge\tau_{C}
.

Since
[\tau_{\partial C}]=[d\beta^{\prime}]\in H_{c}^{n-k}(V)
are representatives of the Thom class with support in
V
, there is
\beta^{\prime\prime}\in\Omega_{c}^{n-k-1}(V)
such that
d\beta^{\prime\prime}=\tau_{\partial C}-d\beta^{\prime}
.
The form
\beta=\beta^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime\prime}
satisfies
d\beta=\tau_{\partial C}
and

\beta|_{U-V}=b\tau_{C}|_{U-V}=\tau_{C}
because on
b=1
on
U-W_{\varepsilon/2}^{\delta}\supset U-V
. This shows that
\beta|_{U-V}
is a Thom form of
C-V
. If
N
intersects
C
transversally on
C-V
then
\beta\wedge\tau_{N}
is a Thom form for
C\cap N
as discussed before.
∎

We now use
[
6
, Proposition 6.19]
to find primitives of Thom forms of certain nullhomologous submanifolds on the fibers of a mapping torus. This will be useful later.

Lemma 9
.

Let
N
be a closed oriented manifold and

F\colon N\to N
be an orientation preserving diffeomorphism.
Let
L_{1},\dots,L_{k}\subset N
be closed oriented submanifolds of dimension
s
, let
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and define
L_{j}^{0}=q(\{t_{0}\}\times L_{j})\subset N_{F}
.

Assume that

\sum_{j}{\lambda_{j}[L_{j}]}=0
on
H^{r}(N,\mathbb{R})
. Consider Thom forms
\tau_{j}
of
L_{j}
on
N
, and
\beta\in\Omega^{*}(N)
such that
d\beta=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\tau_{j}
. There is a function
b\colon[t_{0}-1/2,t_{0}+1/2]\to\mathbb{R}
, which is constant outside a neighborhood of
t_{0}
and such that

1.

\tau_{j}^{0}=db\wedge\tau_{L_{j}}
is a Thom form of
L_{j}^{0}
.

2.

d(-db\wedge\beta)=\sum_{j}{\lambda_{j}}\tau_{j}^{0}
.

Proof.

Observe that
\nu(L_{j}^{0})\cong\langle(-1)^{s}\partial_{t}\rangle\oplus\nu_{N}(L_{j})
as oriented vector bundles. We claim that a Thom form for
\langle(-1)^{s}\partial_{t}\rangle\to q(\{t_{0}\}\times N)
, is
\exp_{N}^{*}(db)
where

b
is a monotonic function
that equals
0
on
[t_{0}-1/2,t_{0}-\varepsilon)
and
(-1)^{s}
if
t\in(t_{0}+\varepsilon,t_{0}+1/2]
. The claim holds because for any
p\in N
we have

\int_{\langle\mathbb{R}(-1)^{s}\partial_{t}|_{p}\rangle}\exp^{*}_{N}(db)=(-1)^{s}\int_{[t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0}+\varepsilon]}{db}=(-1)^{s}(b(t_{0}+\varepsilon)-b(t_{0}-\varepsilon))=1.

In particular,
\exp^{*}_{N}(db)
restricts to a Thom form of
\langle(-1)^{s}\partial_{t}\rangle\to L_{j}^{0}
. Since
db
extends to
N_{F}
, so do the forms

db\wedge\beta
and
db\wedge\tau_{Lj}
. In addition,

[
6
, Proposition 6.19]
ensures that a Thom form of
L_{j}^{0}
is
\tau_{j}^{0}=db\wedge\tau_{j}
. Finally,
d(-db\wedge\beta)=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}db\wedge\tau_{j}=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\tau_{j}^{0}
.
∎

Linking number
: The following discussion is contained in
[
6
, p. 230– 234]
.

Definition 10
.

Let
M
be a compact oriented manifold without boundary and let
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
be two disjoint compact oriented submanifolds without boundary that are nullhomologous (on
H_{*}(M,\mathbb{R})
) and such that
\mathrm{dim}(L_{1})+\mathrm{dim}(L_{2})=\mathrm{dim}(M)-1
. The linking number of
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
is defined as

\mathrm{lk}_{M}(L_{1},L_{2})=\int_{M}{\beta_{1}\wedge\tau_{2}},

where
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
are Thom forms of
L_{j}
and
d\beta_{1}=\tau_{1}
.

The linking number does not depend on the choices of
\tau_{j}
and
\beta_{1}
. Let
\tau_{1}^{\prime}
and
\tau_{2}^{\prime}
be Thom forms of
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
and let
\beta_{1}^{\prime}
with
d\beta_{1}^{\prime}=\tau_{1}^{\prime}
. There are forms
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
supported on tubular neighborhoods of
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
respectively with
d\alpha_{j}=\tau_{j}^{\prime}-\tau_{j}
.
Hence, there is a closed form
c
on
M
such that
\beta_{1}^{\prime}-\beta_{1}=c+\alpha_{1}
.
Using that
\alpha_{1}\wedge\tau_{2}^{\prime}=0
we obtain:

\displaystyle\beta_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\tau_{2}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle(\beta_{1}^{\prime}-\beta_{1})\wedge\tau_{2}^{\prime}+\beta_{1}\wedge\tau_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(c+\alpha_{1})\wedge\tau_{2}^{\prime}+\beta_{1}\wedge(\tau_{2}^{\prime}-\tau_{2})+\beta_{1}\wedge\tau_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\wedge\tau_{2}^{\prime}+\beta_{1}\wedge d\alpha_{2}+\beta_{1}\wedge\tau_{2}.

Since
[\tau_{2}]=0
and
c
is closed,
[c\wedge\tau_{2}]=0
. In addition,
\int_{M}{\beta_{1}\wedge d\alpha_{2}}=\pm\int_{M}{\tau_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}}=0
, as
\tau_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}
vanishes pointwise.
Hence,
\int_{M}{\beta_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\tau_{2}^{\prime}}=\int_{M}{\beta_{1}\wedge\tau_{2}}
.

In the set-up of Definition
10
, if we also suppose that
L_{1}=\partial C
, and
C
intersects
L_{2}
transversally. Then
C\cap L_{1}
is a finite collection of points
\{p_{j}\}
with orientations
\varepsilon_{j}\in\{\pm 1\}
. Lemma
8
ensures that
\mathrm{lk}(L_{1},L_{2})=\sum_{j}\varepsilon_{j}
.

2.4
Resolution of closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
orbifolds

\mathrm{G}_{2}
structures:
The standard cross product
\times\colon\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{R}^{7}\to\mathbb{R}^{7}
, and the scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
determine the
3
-form
\varphi_{0}(u,v,w)=\langle u\times v,w\rangle
. In terms of the standard basis
(e^{1},\dots,e^{7})
of
(\mathbb{R}^{7})^{*}
, is expressed as follows:

\varphi_{0}=e^{123}+e^{145}+e^{167}+e^{246}-e^{25}-e^{347}-e^{357}.

(9)

The group
\mathrm{G}_{2}
is the stabilizer of
\varphi_{0}
under the action of
\mathrm{Gl}(7,\mathbb{R})
on
\Lambda^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{7})^{*}
. It is a simply connected,
14
-dimensional subgroup of
\mathrm{SO}(7)
.
There is an inclusion
\mathrm{SU}(2)\subset\mathrm{G}_{2}
associated with the splitting
\mathbb{R}^{7}=\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{4}
for which
\varphi_{0}=e^{123}+e^{1}\wedge\omega_{1}^{0}+e^{2}\wedge\omega_{2}^{0}-e^{3}\wedge\omega_{3}^{0}
, where

(\omega_{1}^{0},\omega_{2}^{0},\omega_{3}^{0})
is the standard
\mathrm{SU}(2)
structure on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
:

\displaystyle\omega_{1}^{0}=e^{45}+e^{67},\qquad\omega_{2}^{0}=e^{46}-e^{57},\qquad\omega_{3}^{0}=e^{47}+e^{56}.

(10)

Definition 11
.

A
\mathrm{G}_{2}
form on a
7
-dimensional manifold
M

is
\varphi\in\Omega^{3}(M)
such that for every
p\in M
there is an isomorphism
u_{p}\colon T_{p}M\to\mathbb{R}^{7}
that satisfies
u_{p}^{*}(\varphi_{0})=\varphi_{p}
. If
\Gamma
is a finite subgroup of diffeomorphisms acting non-freely, a
\mathrm{G}_{2}
form on the orbifold
M/\Gamma
is a
\Gamma
-invariant
\mathrm{G}_{2}
form on
M
.

A
\mathrm{G}_{2}
form
\varphi
determines an orientation and a metric
g
by the identity,

i(X)\varphi\wedge i(Y)\varphi\wedge\varphi=6g(X,Y)\mathrm{vol}_{g},

(11)

where
\mathrm{vol}_{g}
denotes the unit-length oriented volume form
associated to
g
. A vector cross product is then determined by the equation
\varphi(X,Y,Z)=g(X\times Y,Z)
,
X,Y,Z\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
. A
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure is a triple
(M,\varphi,g)
. According to the classification by Fernandez and Gray,
[
17
]
we say it is closed if
d\varphi=0
, and torsion-free when
\nabla\varphi=0
.

To construct a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
-structure on the resolution of an orbifold, we need to interpolate between closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
forms that are sufficiently close. To ensure that the result is a
\mathrm{G}_{2}
form, we use the following Lemma, which follows from the fact that the orbit of
\varphi_{0}
under the action of
\mathrm{GL}(7,\mathbb{R})
is open in
\Lambda^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{7})^{*}
.

Lemma 12
.

[
23
, Chapter 10,
Section 3]

[
29
, Lemma 2.4]

There exists a universal constant
m
such that if
(M,\varphi,g)
is a
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure and
\|\phi-\varphi\|_{C^{0},g}<m
then
\phi
is a
\mathrm{G}_{2}
form.

Associative submanifolds:

Let
(M,\varphi,g)
be a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure,
a
3
-dimensional submanifold
L
is associative if

\varphi(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})=\pm 1,\text{ for all }p\in L\text{ and for all }(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\text{ orthonormal basis of }T_{p}L.

Of course,
L
is oriented by
\varphi|_{L}
, which is the unit-length volume form induced by the orientation and
g|_{L}
. The group
\mathrm{G}_{2}
acts transitively on the Grassmanian of associative
3
-planes in
\mathbb{R}^{7}
(see
[
33
, Corollary 8.3]
). The local model of an associative submanifold is that of
\mathbb{R}^{3}\subset\mathbb{R}^{7}=\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{4}
described above. This implies, in particular, that
if
L
is associative then

\varphi|_{TM|_{L}}\in\Lambda^{3}(T^{*}L)\oplus\Lambda^{1}T^{*}L\otimes\Lambda^{2}\nu(L)^{*},

(12)

where
\nu(L)
denotes the normal bundle of
L
.
A unit-length vector field
V\in\mathfrak{X}(L)
determines a complex structure on
\nu(L)
by:

X\longmapsto J_{\nu(L)}(X)=V\times X,

(13)

where
\times
is the cross product determined by
\varphi
.
Observe that the associated
2
-form is
\omega(X,Y)=g(J_{\nu(L)}(X),Y)=i(V)\varphi|_{\nu(L)}
.
We now show that the orientation induced by
J_{\nu(L)}
on
\nu(L)
coincides with the one determined by the orientation of
L
and
M
. We complete
v_{1}=V(p)
to an orthonormal oriented basis
(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})
of
T_{p}L
, and choose an orthonormal oriented basis
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})\in\nu_{p}(L)
. Then,
using equations (
11
) and (
12
) we obtain:

\displaystyle 6

\displaystyle=6g(v_{1},v_{1})\mathrm{vol}_{g}(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})=(i(v_{1})\varphi)^{2}\wedge\varphi\,(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})

\displaystyle=(i(v_{1})\varphi)^{3}(v_{2},v_{3},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})=3\varphi(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})(i(v_{1})\varphi)^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})

\displaystyle=3\omega^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4}).

Hence,
\frac{1}{2}\omega^{2}
is the oriented unit-length volume form of
\nu(L)
.

Resolution of compact closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
orbifolds
M/\mathbb{Z}_{2}

Let
(M,\varphi,g)
be a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure, and let
\iota\colon M\to M
be an involution with fixed points such that
\iota^{*}(\varphi)=\varphi
. We now discuss whether we can find a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
resolution
\rho\colon(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\varphi},\widetilde{g})\to(X=M/\iota,\varphi,g)
. By this, we mean a smooth
manifold endowed with a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\varphi},\widetilde{g})
and a map
\rho\colon\widetilde{M}\to X
such that:

1.

The map
\rho\colon\widetilde{M}-\rho^{-1}(L)\to X-L
is a diffeomorphism.

2.

There exists a small neighbourhood
U
of
L
such that
\rho^{*}(\varphi)=\widetilde{\varphi}
on
\widetilde{M}-\rho^{-1}(U)
.

To begin, the next result states that the fixed locus of
\iota
is an associative submanifold:

Lemma 13
.

[
29
, Lemma 2.8]

Let
(M,\varphi,g)
be a
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure and assume that
\iota\colon M\to M
satisfies
\iota^{*}\varphi=\varphi
. Then
\iota
is either fixed point-free or the submanifold
L=\operatorname{Fix}(\iota)
is an associative
3
-dimensional submanifold.

From now on, we assume that
L=\operatorname{Fix}(\iota)\neq\emptyset
, and that
L
is oriented by
\varphi|_{L}
.

The resolution of
M/\iota
occurs on the normal bundle of
L
, where
\iota_{*}
acts as
-\mathrm{Id}
. The choice of a unit-length vector field determines the almost complex structure
J_{\nu(L)}
on
\nu(L)
described by equation (
13
), which is invariant under conjugation by
\iota_{*}
. This allows us to identify the fibers of

\nu(L)/\iota_{*}
with
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
The resolution of
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is
\chi_{0}\colon\widetilde{\mathbb{C}^{2}}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, where

\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2}=\{(z_{1},z_{2},\ell)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}\mbox{ s.t. }(z_{1},z_{2})\in\ell\},

and the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action is
(z_{1},z_{2},\ell)\longmapsto(-z_{1},-z_{2},\ell)
. This space, known as the Eguchi-Hanson space, admits a family of metrics with holonomy
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. Given

a>0
, one such metric is determined by the standard complex structure and the
2
-form:

\widehat{\omega}_{1}^{a}=-\frac{1}{4}dId\left((r_{0}^{4}+a^{2})^{1/2}-a\log((r_{0}^{4}+a^{2})^{1/2}+a)+2a\log(r_{0})\right),

where
r_{0}
denotes the radial function.
These share the complex volume form

\chi_{0}^{*}(\omega_{2}^{0}+i\omega_{3}^{0})
.

The fiberwise blow-up
P\to\nu(L)/\iota_{*}
is a smooth bundle over
L
, with fiber
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}^{2}}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. More precisely, let
P_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(\nu(L))
be the principal
\mathrm{U}(2)
bundle associated to
(\nu(L),J_{\nu(L)})
, we can describe
P
as follows:

P=P_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(\nu(L))\times_{\mathrm{U}(2)}\widetilde{\mathbb{C}^{2}}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{([v_{x}]_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}},\ell_{x})\colon\,(v_{x},\ell_{x})\in\nu_{x}(L)\times\mathbb{P}(\nu_{x}(L)),\,v_{x}\in\ell_{x}\},

(14)

where
[v_{x}]_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
denotes the equivalence class of
v_{x}
under the action of
\iota_{*}|_{\nu(L)}=-\mathrm{Id}
.
The exceptional divisor is
Q=P_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(\nu(L))\times_{\mathrm{U}(2)}\mathbb{CP}^{1}=\mathbb{P}(\nu(L))
.
The manifold
P
carries an orientation such that the projection
P-Q\to\nu(L)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}-L
is orientation-preserving. In addition, the orientation of
Q
is determined by the orientation of
\nu(L)
.

When
L
admits a nowhere-vanishing closed
1
-form, one can construct a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
resolution by replacing a tubular neighborhood of
L
on
X
, with a neighborhood of
Q
in
P
:

Theorem 14
.

[
29
, Section 3]

Let
(M,\varphi,g)
be a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structure on a compact manifold. Suppose that
\iota\colon M\to M
is an involution such that
\iota^{*}\varphi=\varphi
with
L=\operatorname{Fix}(\iota)\neq\emptyset
.
If there is a nowhere-vanishing closed
1
-form
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(L)
, then there exists a closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
resolution of the orbifold
(X=M/\iota,\varphi,g)
.

Remark 3
.

The proof shows the existence of a one-parameter family of closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
structures on the resolution, but this was not stated in the theorem. We will make this explicit in the equivariant version of this method.

According to
[
37
]
, a nowhere-vanishing closed
1
-form exists on the oriented
3
-dimensional manifold
L
if and only if every connected component
L_{j}
is the mapping torus of an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism
f_{j}\colon\Sigma_{j}\to\Sigma_{j}
of an orientable surface
\Sigma_{j}
with genus of
g_{j}
.
We now choose an orientation on
\Sigma_{j}
; since

TL_{j}=\langle\partial_{t}\rangle\oplus\mathcal{V}_{j},\qquad\mathcal{V}_{j}=[0,1]\times T\Sigma_{j}/(0,v_{x})\sim(1,d_{x}f_{j}(v_{x})),

and
TL_{j}
is oriented by
\varphi|_{L_{j}}
, we orient
\mathcal{V}_{j}
so that the equality
TL_{j}=\langle\partial_{t}\rangle\oplus\mathcal{V}_{j}
holds as oriented vector bundles. The fixed orientation is that of
\mathcal{V}_{j}|_{t=0}=T\Sigma_{j}
.

We now recall some facts needed in subsection
3.1
. From the short exact sequence (
7
) and the equality
H^{3}(L_{j})=\mathbb{R}
, it follows that
f_{j}^{*}-\mathrm{Id}=0
on
H^{2}(\Sigma_{j})
. Hence, a volume form
\mathrm{vol}_{j}
on
\Sigma_{j}

that integrates to
1
determines, by equation (
8
), a closed form
\omega_{j}\in\Omega^{2}(L_{j})
that yields a non-zero cohomology class. In addition, the splitting
TL_{j}=\langle\partial_{t}\rangle\oplus\mathcal{V}_{j}
might not be orthogonal, but an orthogonal orientation-preserving splitting is given by

TL=\langle e_{1}\rangle\oplus\mathcal{V}_{j},\qquad e_{1}=\frac{dt^{\sharp}}{\|dt\|},

(15)

because
\ker(dt)=\mathcal{V}_{j}
and
g(e_{1},\partial_{t})>0
. Indeed,
J_{\mathcal{V}_{j}}\colon{\mathcal{V}_{j}}\to{\mathcal{V}_{j}}
,
J_{\mathcal{V}_{j}}(W)=e_{1}\times W
, is an almost complex structure compatible with the orientation.
Finally, the diffeomorphism type of
L_{j}
depends on the class of the mapping diffeomorphism
f_{j}
in the quotient
\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(\Sigma_{j})/\mathrm{Isot}_{0}(\Sigma_{j})
. Here
\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(\Sigma_{j})
is the group of orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms and
\mathrm{Isot}_{0}(\Sigma_{j})
is the subgroup of diffeomorphisms isotopic to the identity.

3
Topological properties of resolutions of compact closed
\mathrm{G}_{2}
orbifolds
M/\mathbb{Z}_{2}

In this section, we compute the cohomology algebra of the resolution, certain triple Massey products arising from the exceptional divisor, and the real Pontryagin class.
We work under the hypotheses of Theorem
37
, and we follow the notation and assumptions in section
2.4
.
In particular, we highlight that the fixed locus
L
is assumed to be oriented by
\varphi|_{L}
. Since the connected components are mapping tori, we also assume that the form used to construct the resolution is
dt
.
Remarks
8
and
10
show how the formulas change if a different closed 1-form is chosen.

3.1
Cohomology algebra of the resolution

This discussion is based on
[
29
, section 4]
,
with minor corrections to
[
29
, Lemma 4.2, Proposition 4.3, Proposition 4.5]
.
We first comment on
[
29
, Proposition 4.1, Lemma 4.2]
. First,
[
29
, Proposition 4.1]
shows that the real first Chern class of
\nu(L_{j})
with respect to the complex structure determined by
e_{1}=\frac{dt^{\sharp}}{\|dt\|}
through equation (
13
) is

c_{1}(\nu(L_{j}))=(2-2g_{j})[\omega_{j}].

We sketch the proof, since it will be used later. First, one shows that
\nu(L_{j})
is complex isomorphic to
\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{j}
, where
\underline{\mathbb{C}}
is the trivial line bundle. The complex structures considered on
\mathcal{V}_{j}
is that induced by
e_{1}
. The trivial factor is determined by a unit-length section
\eta\colon L_{j}\to\nu(L_{j})
, which exists because the rank of the bundle is higher than the dimension of the manifold. The explicit isomorphism is:

A\colon\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{j}\to\nu(L_{j}),\qquad A(u+iv,W_{p})=u\,\eta(p)+v\,e_{1}(p)\times\eta(p)+W_{p}\times\eta(p).

This preserves the complex structure because

(e_{1}\times W_{p})\times\eta(p)=e_{1}\times(W_{p}\times\eta(p))
(see
[
33
, Lemma 2.6]
). Hence,
c_{1}(\nu(L_{j}))=c_{1}(\mathcal{V}_{j})
. Recall that for complex line bundles, the first Chern class coincides with the Euler class of the bundle, viewed as an oriented real rank 2 bundle. We compute the Euler class
e(\mathcal{V}_{j})
by
pulling back a Thom form of
\mathcal{V}_{j}\to L_{j}
via the zero section
s\colon L_{j}\to\mathcal{V}_{j}
. This Thom form is obtained from a Thom form
\upsilon_{j}
of
T\Sigma_{j}\to\Sigma_{j}

using that
\mathcal{V}_{j}
is the mapping torus of
df_{j}\colon T\Sigma_{j}\to T\Sigma_{j}
. More precisely,

(df_{j})^{*}(\upsilon_{j})
is another Thom form of
T\Sigma_{j}\to\Sigma_{j}
because
df_{j}
preserves the orientation of the fibers. Hence,
[\upsilon_{j}]\in\ker((df_{j})^{*}-\mathrm{Id})
, and equation (
8
) allows us to extend
\upsilon_{j}
:

\tau_{j}=(df_{j})^{*}(\upsilon_{j})+d(\rho(t)\beta_{j})\in\Omega_{c}^{2}(\mathcal{V}_{j}),\mbox{ where }d\beta_{j}=\upsilon_{j}-(df_{j})^{*}\upsilon_{j}.

This is a Thom form because it integrates to
1
over the fibers.
Using that
s[t,p]=[t,s_{0}(p)]
, where
s_{0}\colon\Sigma_{j}\to T\Sigma_{j}
is the zero section, and
df_{j}\circ s_{0}=s_{0}\circ f_{j}
one deduces that

s^{*}(\tau_{j})=f_{j}^{*}(s_{0}^{*}(\upsilon_{j}))+d(\rho(t)s_{0}^{*}(\beta_{j})).

The right hand side is the closed form determined by
s_{0}^{*}(\upsilon_{j})
using equation (
8
). The claimed identity follows from
[s_{0}^{*}(\upsilon_{j})]=c_{1}(\Sigma_{j})=(2-2g_{j})[\mathrm{vol}_{j}]
.

The proof of
[
29
, Lemma 4.2]
requires that the first Chern class, in
H^{2}(L_{j},\mathbb{Z})
, is even. This holds, in particular, if the Chern class of
\nu(L_{j})
coincides with
(2-2g_{j})PD[S^{1}]
, where a
S^{1}
is a vertical loop (for instance,
t\mapsto[t,x]
for some fixed point
x\in\Sigma_{j}
of
f_{j}\colon\Sigma_{j}\to\Sigma_{j}
). However, this is not guaranteed by the the proof of
[
29
, Proposition 4,1]
, which is why we now introduce an additional hypothesis.

Lemma 15
.

[
29
, Correction of Lemma 4.2]

If the first Chern class of
\nu(L_{j})
is even,
then
Q_{j}
is a trivial
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
bundle.

This Lemma holds in two special cases that are of particular interest to us: when
\Sigma_{j}=T^{2}
, and when
f_{j}
is isotopic to the identity.

1.

If
\Sigma_{j}=T^{2}
, we claim that
\mathcal{V}_{j}
is trivial as a real oriented bundle, and hence its first Chern class vanishes.
To check this, we set
T^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
,
so that
\mathrm{Diff}^{+}(T^{2})/\mathrm{Iso}_{0}(T^{2})\cong\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
. We therefore assume
f_{j}[x_{1},x_{2}]=[A(x_{1},x_{2})]
for some
A\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
.
Let
A_{s}
be a path in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
A_{0}=\mathrm{Id}
and
A_{1}=A
. Then

E_{s}=[0,1]\times T^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}/(0,x,w)\sim(1,A(x),A_{s}(w)),

defines a homotopy between the trivial bundle
E_{0}
and
\mathcal{V}_{j}
, and hence these bundles are isomorphic.

2.

If
f_{j}
is isotopic to the identity, then
L_{j}=S^{1}\times\Sigma_{j}
so that
\mathcal{V}_{j}
is the pullback of
T\Sigma_{j}
. Its first Chern class is then
(2-2g_{j})PD[S^{1}]
, which is even.

We now fix a tubular neighborhood
U_{j}
of
L_{j}
, and set
O_{j}=\rho^{-1}(U_{j})
. Let
\mathrm{pr}_{j}\colon O_{j}\to L_{j}
denote the composition of
\rho
and the nearest point projection
\bar{\pi}_{j}\colon U_{j}\to L_{j}
: this is a smooth map. We denote by
\tau_{j}
a Thom form of
Q_{j}
supported in
O_{j}
.

Since
Q_{j}=\mathbb{P}(\nu(L_{j}))
,
the standard theory of Chern classes (see
[
6
, p. 269–271]
) allows us to find the cohomology algebra of
Q_{j}
.

Proposition 16
.

[
29
, Correction of Proposition 4.3]

Consider the tautological line bundle over
Q_{j}
,

S_{j}=\{(v_{x},\ell_{x})\in\nu_{x}(L_{j})\times\mathbb{P}(\nu_{x}(L_{j})),\,v_{x}\in\ell_{x}\},

and let

\mathrm{e}_{j}\in H^{2}(Q_{j})
be its Euler class. Denote by
H^{*}(L_{j})[\mathbf{y}_{j}]
the algebra of polynomials with coeffiecients in
H^{*}(L_{j})
.
Then,

F_{j}\colon H^{*}(L_{j})[\mathbf{y}_{j}]/\langle\mathbf{y}^{2}_{j}+(2-2g_{j})[\omega_{j}]\mathbf{y}_{j}\rangle\to H^{*}(Q_{j}),\qquad F_{j}(\beta)=\mathrm{pr}^{*}\beta|_{Q_{j}},\quad F_{j}(\mathbf{y}_{j})=-\mathrm{e}_{j},

is an isomorphism of algebras.

Remark 4
.

There is a typo in
[
29
, Proposition 4.3]
, where we defined
F(\mathbf{y}_{j})=\mathrm{e}_{j}
.

Remark 5
.

We now argue that
\mathrm{e}_{j}=\frac{1}{2}[\tau_{j}]|_{Q_{j}}
.
Let
\mathrm{q}_{j}\colon S_{j}\to P_{j}
be the quotient projection, we claim that the Thom form of the bundle
S_{j}\to Q_{j}
equals
\frac{1}{2}[\mathrm{q}_{j}^{*}(\tau_{j})]
. This holds because the fiber of the normal bundle of
Q_{j}
in
S_{j}
at a point
x\in Q_{j}
is
F_{x}=\mathbb{C}
, whereas in
P_{j}
it is
F_{x}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, hence:

\int_{F_{x}}{\mathrm{q}_{j}^{*}(\tau_{j})}=2\int_{F_{x}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}}{\tau_{j}}=2.

Therefore,
\mathrm{e}_{j}=\frac{1}{2}[\mathrm{q}_{j}^{*}\tau_{j}]|_{Q_{j}}=\frac{1}{2}[\tau_{j}]|_{Q_{j}}
. This is consistent with the fact that
P_{j}|_{x}\to Q_{j}|_{x}\cong\mathbb{CP}^{1}
is a line bundle, isomorphic to
S_{j}|_{x}\otimes S_{j}|_{x}
.

Concerning the cohomology groups of the resolution, we have the following result

Proposition 17
.

[
29
, Proposition 4.4]

There is a split short exact sequence:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{*}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.02618pt\raise 6.35265pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\rho^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 90.06943pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 90.06943pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{*}(\widetilde{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 126.97122pt\raise 6.15279pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.18057pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\oplus_{j}r_{j}\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 8.19447pt}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 147.125pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 147.125pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigoplus_{j}H^{*}(Q_{j})/H^{*}(L_{j})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 268.03229pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 268.03229pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
r_{j}
denotes the restriction
H^{*}(\widetilde{X})\to H^{*}(Q_{j})
followed by projection to the quotient.

The cohomology of
X
is calculated from
(\Omega(X),d)=(\Omega(M)^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}},d)
(see
[
29
, Section 2.1]
). Similarly to

[
30
, Lemma 5]
, and using the Poincaré’s Lemma, representatives of cohomology classes can be chosen such that their pullbacks are smooth. More generally, one can prove the following:

Lemma 18
.

[
30
, Lemma 5]

Let
U_{j}^{\prime}\subset U_{j}
be a tubular neighborhood of
L_{j}
, and let
\alpha\in\Omega^{k}(X)
.
If
d\beta|_{\sqcup U_{j}^{\prime}}=0
, then there is
\beta\in\Omega^{k-1}(X)
, supported in
\sqcup U_{j}^{\prime}
such that
\rho^{*}(\alpha+d\beta)
is smooth.

In addition, Proposition
16
implies:

H^{*}(Q_{j})/H^{*}(L_{j})\cong H^{*-2}(L_{j})\otimes\langle\mathbf{x}_{j}\rangle,\qquad\deg(\mathbf{x}_{j})=2.

Here,
\mathbf{x}_{j}=-2\mathbf{y}_{j}
, so that
F_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j})=2\mathrm{e}_{j}=[\tau_{j}]|_{Q_{j}}
by Remark
5
.
A splitting is

H^{*-2}(L_{j})\otimes\langle\mathbf{x}_{j}\rangle\to H^{*}(\widetilde{X}),\qquad[\beta]\otimes\mathbf{x}_{j}\longmapsto[\mathrm{pr}_{j}^{*}\beta\wedge\tau_{j}].

Regarding the product structure on
H^{*}(\widetilde{X})
, we now amend a minor error in
[
29
, Proposition 4.5]
. More precisely, we now check:

[\tau_{j}^{2}]=-2\rho^{*}(\mathrm{Th}[L_{j}])+(4-4g_{j})[\mathrm{pr}_{j}^{*}(\omega_{j})\wedge\tau_{j}].

(16)

According to Proposition
16
,
[\tau_{j}^{2}]|_{Q_{j}}=F_{j}(4\mathbf{y}_{j}^{2})=(4-4g_{j})F_{j}([\omega_{j}](-2\mathbf{y}_{j}))=(4-4g_{j})[\mathrm{pr}_{j}^{*}(\omega_{j})\wedge\tau_{j}]|_{Q_{j}}
. From Proposition
17
we deduce,

[\tau_{j}^{2}-(4-4g_{j})\mathrm{pr_{j}}^{*}\omega_{j}\wedge\tau_{j}]\in\mathrm{Im}(\rho^{*}).

We construct a new representative supported in
U_{j}
that vanishes around
Q_{j}
.
By Poincaré’s Lemma, there is
\beta_{j}\in\Omega^{3}(O_{j})
such that
d\beta_{j}=\left(\tau_{j}^{2}-(4-4g_{j})\mathrm{pr}_{j}^{*}\omega_{j}\wedge\tau_{j}\right)|_{O_{j}}
.
Let
b
be a bump function supported in
O_{j}
, equal to
1
on a smaller tubular neighborhood
V_{j}\subset O_{j}
. The representative is

\gamma=\tau_{j}^{2}-(4-4g_{j})\mathrm{pr_{j}}^{*}\omega_{j}\wedge\tau_{j}-d(b\beta_{j}).

The pushfoward
\rho_{*}\gamma
is then well-defined, and
[\rho_{*}\gamma]\in H^{4}_{c}(U_{j})
. Equation (
16
) will be proved once we show
[\rho_{*}\gamma]=-2\mathrm{Th}[L_{j}]
because
\rho^{*}[\rho_{*}\gamma]=[\tau_{j}^{2}-(4-4g_{j})\mathrm{pr_{j}}^{*}\omega_{j}\wedge\tau_{j}]
.
For that purpose, it is sufficient to check that

\int_{\nu_{x}(L_{j})/\iota_{*}}{\rho_{*}(\gamma)}=-2
, for all
x\in L_{j}
. Using that
\mathrm{pr}_{j}^{*}(\omega_{j})|_{P_{x}}=0
, and that
\tau_{j}|_{Q_{x}}
is the Thom form of
Q_{x}\subset P_{x}
we obtain,

\int_{\nu_{x}(L_{j})/\iota_{*}}{\rho_{*}\gamma}=\int_{\nu_{x}(L_{j})/\iota_{*}-\{0_{x}\}}{\rho_{*}\gamma}=\int_{P_{x}-Q_{x}}{\gamma}=\int_{P_{x}}{\tau_{j}^{2}}=\int_{Q_{x}}{\tau_{j}}=[Q_{x}][Q_{x}]=-2.

This finishes the proof of formula (
16
), and yields to:

Proposition 19
.

[
29
, Correction of Proposition 4.5]

There is an isomorphism

H^{*}(\widetilde{X})=H^{*}(X)\bigoplus\oplus_{j}H^{*-2}(L_{j})\otimes\langle\mathbf{x}_{j}\rangle.

Let
[\alpha],[\beta]\in H^{*}(X)
,
[\gamma_{j}],[\gamma_{j}^{\prime}]\in H^{*}(L_{j})
. The wedge product on
H^{*}(\widetilde{X})
determines the following product on the left hand side:

1.

[\alpha]\cdot[\beta]=[\alpha\wedge\beta]
,

2.

[\alpha]\cdot[\gamma_{j}]\otimes\mathbf{x}_{j}=[\alpha|_{L_{j}}\wedge\gamma_{j}]\otimes\mathbf{x}_{j}
,

3.

[\gamma_{j}]\otimes\mathbf{x}_{j}\cdot[\gamma_{k}^{\prime}]\otimes\mathbf{x}_{k}=0
if
j\neq k
,

4.

[\gamma_{j}]\otimes\mathbf{x}_{j}\cdot[\gamma_{j}^{\prime}]\otimes\mathbf{x}_{j}=-2\,[\bar{\pi}_{j}^{*}(\gamma_{j}\wedge\gamma_{j}^{\prime})]\wedge\mathrm{Th}[L_{j}]+(4-4g_{j})[\omega_{j}]\otimes\mathbf{x}_{j}
.

Remark 6
.

When
g_{j}=1
, we argued that
\nu(L_{j})=L_{j}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Then, similar to the discussion in
[
30
, Section 3.2]
, letting
r=\|z\|
we can write:

\tau_{j}=\frac{1}{\pi i}d(b(r^{2})\partial\log(r^{2}))=\frac{2}{\pi i}b^{\prime}(r^{2})rdr\wedge\partial\log(r^{2})+\frac{b(r^{2})}{\pi i}\overline{\partial}{\partial}\log r^{2},

(17)

where
b(r)
is an increasing function that equals
-1
on
r\leq\varepsilon^{2}
and

0
on
r\geq 4\varepsilon^{2}
.
From this, one deduces that
\tau^{2}_{j}
vanishes on a tubular neighborhood of
L_{j}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}
. Therefore,
\rho_{*}(\tau^{2}_{j})
is well-defined and a similar argument shows that
-\frac{1}{2}\rho_{*}(\tau^{2}_{j})
is a Thom form of
L_{j}
.

Remark 7
.

Assume that the components
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
of the singular locus satisfy
g_{1}=g_{2}
,
\mathrm{Th}[L_{1}]=\mathrm{Th}[L_{2}]
and there is
[\alpha]\in H^{2}(X)
such that
[\alpha|_{L_{j}}]=[\omega_{j}]
if
g_{1}\neq 1
; otherwise we consider
\alpha=0
. Then, Proposition
19
implies:

(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2})\cdot(\mathbf{x}_{1}+\mathbf{x}_{2}-(4-4g_{1})[\alpha])=\mathbf{x}_{1}^{2}-(4-4g_{1})[\omega_{1}]\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1}-(\mathbf{x}^{2}-(4-4g_{2})[\omega_{2}]\otimes\mathbf{x}_{2})=-2\mathrm{Th}[L_{1}]+2\mathrm{Th}[L_{2}]=0.

Remark 8
.

If we instead construct the resolution using
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(L)
, closed and nowhere-vanishing,
there is an isomorphism
\nu(L_{j})\cong\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\ker(\theta|_{L_{j}})
, where the second summand is endowed with the complex structure determined by
X\mapsto\frac{\theta^{\sharp}}{\|\theta\|}\times X
. Hence,
c_{1}(\nu(L_{j}))=c_{1}(\ker(\theta)|_{L_{j}})
. One can then follow the argument replacing
(2-2g_{j})[\omega_{j}]
with
c_{1}(\ker(\theta))
, and obtain the product formula

[\gamma_{j}]\otimes\mathbf{x}_{j}\cdot[\gamma_{j}^{\prime}]\otimes\mathbf{x}_{j}=-2\,[\bar{\pi}_{j}^{*}(\gamma_{j}\wedge\gamma_{j}^{\prime})]\wedge\mathrm{Th}[L_{j}]+2\,c_{1}(\ker(\theta|_{L_{j}}))\otimes\mathbf{x}_{j},

where
\mathbf{x}_{j}
represents the Thom class of the exceptional divisor.

3.2
Triple Massey products

The following result provides a geometric criterion for the vanishing of certain triple Massey products. We denote by
-L_{j}
to the submanifold
L_{j}
equipped with the reversed orientation, i.e, the one induced by
-\varphi|_{L_{j}}
.

Proposition 20
.

Let
L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4}
be different connected components of
L
diffeomorphic to a mapping torus with fiber
T^{2}
, and such that
PD[L_{1}]=PD[L_{2}]
and
PD[L_{3}]=PD[L_{4}]
on
H^{4}(X)
.

If
\mathrm{lk}_{M}(L_{3}\sqcup-L_{4},L_{1}\sqcup-L_{2})\neq 0
, then

\langle\mathrm{Th}[Q_{1}]+\mathrm{Th}[Q_{2}],\mathrm{Th}[Q_{3}]+\mathrm{Th}[Q_{4}],\mathrm{Th}[Q_{3}]-\mathrm{Th}[Q_{4}]\rangle

does not vanish.

Proof.

The linking number is well-defined because
L_{1}\sqcup-L_{2}
and
L_{3}\sqcup-L_{4}
are nullhomologous on
H_{3}(M,\mathbb{R})
as a consequence of Remark
2
. The triple Massey product is well-defined according to Proposition
19
and Remark
7
. To compute it, we pick the representatives
\tau_{j}
of
\mathrm{Th}[Q_{j}]
provided by equation (
17
).
Let
\beta\in\Omega^{3}(M)^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
such that

d\beta=\rho_{*}(\tau_{3}^{2}-\tau_{4}^{2}).

Since both
\rho_{*}\tau_{3}^{2}
and
\rho_{*}\tau_{4}^{2}
vanish on a neighborhood of the singular locus (see Remark
6
), Lemma
18
allows us to assume that

\rho^{*}(\beta)
is smooth. The triple Massey product does not vanish if and only if

[\gamma]=[(\tau_{1}+\tau_{2})\wedge\rho^{*}\beta]\notin\mathcal{I},\mbox{ where }\mathcal{I}=[\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}]H^{4}(\widetilde{X})+[\tau_{3}-\tau_{4}]H^{4}(\widetilde{X}).

Observe that if
[\gamma]\wedge[\tau_{1}-\tau_{2}]=[(\tau_{1}^{2}-\tau_{2}^{2})\wedge\rho^{*}(\beta)]\neq 0
, then
[\gamma]\notin\mathcal{I}
because

[\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}]\wedge[\tau_{1}-\tau_{2}]=0
by Remark
7
and

(\tau_{3}-\tau_{4})\wedge(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})=0
. The result is a consequence of the fact that
\int_{\widetilde{X}}{\gamma\wedge(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})}
and
\mathrm{lk}_{M}(L_{3}\sqcup-L_{4},L_{1}\sqcup-L_{2})
are proportional, which we now prove. We first observe:

\int_{\widetilde{X}}{\gamma\wedge(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})}=\int_{X}{\rho_{*}(\tau_{1}^{2}-\tau_{2}^{2})\wedge\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}{\rho_{*}(\tau_{1}^{2}-\tau_{2}^{2})\wedge\beta}.

Moreover,
\upsilon_{j}=-\frac{1}{2}\rho_{*}(\tau_{j}^{2})
is a Thom form for
L_{j}
by Remark
2
. Remark
6
implies that
-\frac{1}{4}\rho_{*}(\tau_{j}^{2})
is a Thom form for
L_{j}
in
M
.
Hence, for
(j,k)\in\{(1,2),(3,4)\}
, we have

\mathrm{Th}[L_{j}\sqcup-L_{k}]=\mathrm{Th}[L_{j}]-\mathrm{Th}[L_{k}]=-\frac{1}{4}[\rho_{*}(\tau_{j}^{2}-\tau_{k}^{2})],

on
M
, and
-\frac{1}{4}\beta
is a primitive of the representative
-\frac{1}{4}\rho_{*}(\tau_{3}^{2}-\tau_{4}^{2})
of
\mathrm{Th}[L_{3}\sqcup-L_{4}]
. Hence,

\mathrm{lk}(L_{3}\sqcup-L_{4},L_{1}\sqcup-L_{2})=\frac{1}{16}\,\int_{M}{\beta\wedge\rho_{*}(\tau_{1}^{2}-\tau_{2}^{2})}=\frac{1}{8}\int_{\widetilde{X}}{\gamma\wedge(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})}.

∎

3.3
First Pontryagin class

To compute
p_{1}(\widetilde{X})
we adapt an argument from
[
19
]
, originally used to
obtain the Chern classes of a blow-up in complex and symplectic geometry. We use some basic properties of the real Pontryagin classes, defined as
p_{k}(E)=(-1)^{k}c_{2k}(E\otimes\mathbb{C})
, that can be found in

[
6
, p. 285–291]
(but note that they use a different sign convention). Note that
c(E\otimes\mathbb{C})=1+\sum_{k\geq 1}(-1)^{k}p_{k}(E)
satisfies
the Withney product formula, naturality under pullback, and the identity
c(E\otimes\mathbb{C})=c(E)c(E^{*})
for any complex bundle
E\to B
, where
E^{*}
denotes the hermitian dual bundle. The last formula implies
p_{1}(E)=c_{1}(E)^{2}-2c_{2}(E)
, using
c_{k}(E^{*})=(-1)^{k}c(E)
. It is also convenient to denote
\tilde{p}(M)=c(TM\otimes\mathbb{C})
, when
M
is a smooth manifold.
We also use two Lemmas, the first is well-known, but the proof is included for completeness. The second is specific to our context, and its proof mimicks a method used to compute the Chern classes of
\mathbb{CP}^{n}
(see
[
6
, pages 280–281]
).

Lemma 21
.

Let
X
be a smooth manifold. Let
Y=\sqcup Y_{j}
be a disjoint union of submanifolds with codimension
r
, and let
\tau_{j}
be a Thom form for
Y_{j}
supported on a tubular neighborhood
B_{\delta}(Y_{j})
. Consider the nearest point projection
\mathrm{pr}_{Y}\colon\sqcup B_{\delta}(Y_{j})\to Y
, then

H^{*}(X,X-Y)\cong\oplus_{j}\mathrm{pr}^{*}H^{*-r}(Y_{j})\wedge[\tau_{j}].

Proof.

Applying excision (taking the open set to be
X-\sqcup D_{\delta}(Y_{j})
, where
D_{\delta}(Y_{j})
denotes the closure of
B_{\delta}(Y_{j})
), and using the fact that
D_{\delta}(Y_{j})-Y_{j}
retracts to the sphere
S_{\delta}(Y_{j})
, we obtain

H^{*}(X,X-Y)=\oplus_{j}H^{*}(D_{\delta}(Y_{j}),D_{\delta}(Y_{j})-Y_{j})=\oplus_{j}H^{*}(D_{\delta}(Y_{j}),S_{\delta}(Y_{j}))=\oplus_{j}H_{c}^{*}(B_{\delta}(Y_{j})).

Finally, Thom isomorphism theorem ensures
H_{c}^{*}(B_{\delta}(Y_{j}))\cong\mathrm{pr}^{*}H^{*-r}(Y_{j})\wedge[\tau_{j}]
.
∎

Lemma 22
.

Let
\pi\colon E\to L
be a complex vector bundle of complex rank
r
. Denote
Q=\mathbb{P}(E)
and
\pi\colon Q\to L
the projection map. Let
S\to Q
be the tautological line bundle and let
\mathrm{e}
be its Euler class. Then,

\tilde{p}(Q)=\pi^{*}(\tilde{p}(L))\left(\sum_{j,k=0}^{r}{(-1)^{j+k}\pi^{*}(c_{j}(E)c_{k}(E))(1-\mathrm{e})^{r-j}(1+\mathrm{e})^{r-k}}\right).

(18)

In particular, if
L
is
3
-dimensional, then

p_{1}(Q)=0,\quad\mbox{ if r=2},\qquad p_{1}(Q)=3\mathrm{e}^{2}-2\pi^{*}(c_{1}(E))\mathrm{e},\quad\mbox{ if r=3}.

(19)

Proof.

The short exact sequence,

0\to\ker(d\pi)\to TQ\to\pi^{*}(TL)\to 0,

yields
\tilde{p}(Q)=c(\ker(d\pi)\otimes\mathbb{C})\pi^{*}(\tilde{p}(L))
. We now claim that
\ker(d\pi)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(S,E^{\prime})

where
E^{\prime}
is the cokernel of the inclusion
S\hookrightarrow\pi^{*}(E)
, which can be identified (once a hermitian metric on
E
is fixed) with

E^{\prime}=\{(v_{x},\ell_{x})\in E_{x}\times Q_{x},v_{x}\perp\ell_{x}\}.

The isomorphism is

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(S,E^{\prime})\to\ker(d\pi),\quad F\colon S_{\ell_{x}}\to E_{\ell_{x}}^{\prime}\longmapsto\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}[v_{x}+tF(v_{x})],\quad\mbox{ if }0\neq v_{x}\in\ell_{x}.

Tensoring the short exact sequence
0\to S\to\pi^{*}(E)\to E^{\prime}\to 0
with
S^{*}
we obtain,

0\to\underline{\mathbb{C}}\to S^{*}\otimes\pi^{*}E\to\ker(d\pi)\to 0.

Hence
c(\ker(d\pi)\otimes\mathbb{C})=c(S^{*}\otimes\pi^{*}E\otimes\mathbb{C})=c(S^{*}\otimes\pi^{*}E)c(S\otimes(\pi^{*}E)^{*})
.
Applying the formula for the Chern classes of the tensor product of two bundles (see for instance
[
6
, formula (21.10), page 279]
),
we obtain:

c(S^{*}\otimes\pi^{*}E)=\sum_{k=0}^{r}{\pi^{*}(c_{k}(E))(1-\mathrm{e})^{r-k}},\quad c(S\otimes(\pi^{*}E)^{*})=\sum_{k=0}^{r}{(-1)^{k}\pi^{*}(c_{k}(E))(1+\mathrm{e})^{r-k}},

where
c_{0}(E)=1
.
Equation (
18
) follows from
\tilde{p}(Q)=c(S^{*}\otimes\pi^{*}E)c(S\otimes(\pi^{*}E)^{*})\pi^{*}(\tilde{p}(L))
. If
L
is
3
-dimensional, then
c(E)=1+c_{1}(E)
,
c_{1}(E)^{2}=0
, and
\tilde{p}(L)=1
. Equation (
18
) yields,

\tilde{p}(Q)=(1-\mathrm{e}^{2})^{r-1}(1-\mathrm{e}^{2}+2\mathrm{e}\,\pi^{*}(c_{1}(E))).

If
r=2
, using that
\mathrm{e}^{2}-\pi^{*}(c_{1}(E))\mathrm{e}=0
(by
[
6
, p. 269–271]
) and
\dim(Q)=5
(so that
\mathrm{e}^{j}=0
for
j\geq 3
), we obtain

\tilde{p}(Q)=1-2\mathrm{e}^{2}+2\pi^{*}(c_{1}(E))\mathrm{e}=1
. Similarly, if
r=3
then
\dim(Q)=7
(so that
\mathrm{e}^{j}=\pi^{*}(c_{1}(E))\mathrm{e}^{j-1}=0
for
j\geq 4
), and

\tilde{p}(Q)=(1-2\mathrm{e}^{2})(1-\mathrm{e}^{2}+2\mathrm{e}\,\pi^{*}(c_{1}(E)))=1-3\mathrm{e}^{2}+2\pi^{*}(c_{1}(E))\mathrm{e}.
Equation (
19
) follows from this.
∎

We let
\mathcal{M}=M\times(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}\langle 1,i\rangle)
, and define the involution
\iota_{\mathcal{M}}=(\iota,-\mathrm{Id})
. The singular locus of
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{M}/\iota_{\mathcal{M}}
is
\mathcal{L}=\sqcup L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}
, with
L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}=L_{j}\times\{\bar{\varepsilon}\}
, where
L_{j}
is a singular component of
M/\iota
, and
\bar{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{Z}\langle\frac{1}{2},\frac{i}{2}\rangle/\mathbb{Z}\langle 1,i\rangle
. We endow
\mathcal{M}
with the product orientation, and orient
L_{j,\varepsilon}
as
L_{j}
. Note that
\nu(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})=(\nu(L_{j})\times\{\bar{\varepsilon}\})\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}
as oriented vector bundles.

A tubular neighborhood of
\mathcal{L}
in the orbifold is diffeomorphic to a neighborhood of the zero section of the orbibundle
\nu(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, where
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{\pm\mathrm{Id}\}
. Using the notation introduced in section
2.4
, this can be described as

\nu(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong P_{U(2)}(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})\times_{\mathrm{U}(2)}\mathbb{C}^{3}/\mathbb{Z}_{2},\qquad P_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})=P_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(\nu(L_{j}))\times\{\bar{\varepsilon}\}.

Of course,
\mathrm{U}(2)\subset\mathrm{U}(3)
in the standard way. We resolve
\nu(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
by blowing-up the zero section. Namely, we replace the zero section with a neighborhood of
\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}=P_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})\times_{\mathrm{U}(2)}\mathbb{CP}^{2}
on
\mathcal{P}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}=P_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})\times_{\mathrm{U}(2)}\widetilde{\mathbb{C}^{3}}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, where

\widetilde{\mathbb{C}^{3}}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{(z,\ell)\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\times\mathbb{CP}^{2},z\in\ell\}/(z,\ell)\sim(-z,\ell).

Set
\mathcal{P}=\sqcup_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{P}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}
and
\mathcal{Q}=\sqcup_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}
. The orientation of

\mathcal{P}
is such that diffeomorphism
\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{Q}\to\nu(\mathcal{L})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}-\mathcal{L}
preserves orientations, the orientation of
\mathcal{Q}
is determined by the orientation of
\nu(\mathcal{L})
.
The fiberwise blow-up produces a resolution
\rho\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{X}
.
Recall that the strict transform of
\mathcal{Z}^{\vee}\subset\mathcal{X}
is the closure of
\rho^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}^{\vee}-\mathcal{L})
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
.
The strict transform of
M\times\{0\}/\iota_{\mathcal{M}}
,
is diffeomorphic to
\widetilde{X}
due to the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(L_{j,\bar{0}})\times_{\mathrm{U}(2)}\widetilde{\mathbb{C}^{3}}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(L_{j,\bar{0}})\times_{\mathrm{U}(2)}\mathbb{C}^{3}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(\nu(L_{j}))\times_{\mathrm{U}(2)}\widetilde{\mathbb{C}^{2}}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(\nu(L_{j}))\times_{\mathrm{U}(2)}\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}.}

(20)

To ease notations we identify
X
and
M
with
M\times\{0\}/\iota_{\mathcal{M}}
and
M\times\{0\}
respectively. We also denote by
\widetilde{X}
to the strict transform of
M\times\{0\}/\iota_{\mathcal{M}}
. We fix a tubular neighborhood
B_{\delta}(\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})
of
\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}
, and we
denote
\mathrm{pr}\colon\sqcup B_{\delta}(\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})\to\sqcup L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}
to the composition of the nearest point projection to
\mathrm{pr}_{\mathcal{Q}}\colon\sqcup B_{\delta}(\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})\to\mathcal{Q}
, and the resolution map
\rho|_{\mathcal{Q}}\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{L}
.

Let
\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}
be a Thom form of
\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}
supported on
B_{\delta}(\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})
. Observe that

\upsilon_{j,\bar{0}}|_{\widetilde{X}}
is a Thom form for
Q_{j}
on
\widetilde{X}
because
P_{j}|_{x}=(\mathcal{P}_{j,\bar{0}})|_{x}
if
x\in Q_{j}
.
Thus

[\upsilon_{j,\bar{0}}^{2}]|_{\widetilde{X}}=[\tau_{j}^{2}]=-2\rho^{*}(\mathrm{Th}[L_{j}])+(4-4g_{j})[\mathrm{pr}_{j}^{*}\omega_{j}\wedge\tau_{j}].

(21)

Similar to Proposition
16
, using
\nu(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})\cong\nu(L_{j})\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}
, and denoting by
[\omega_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]
the class determined by the unit-length volume form of the fiber (see section
2.4
), we obtain:

Proposition 23
.

Consider the tautological line bundle

\mathcal{S}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}=\{(v_{x},\ell_{x})\in\nu_{x}(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})\times\mathbb{P}(\nu_{x}(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})),\quad v_{x}\in\ell_{x}\}\to\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}},

and let
\mathrm{e}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}
be its Euler class. Denote by
H^{*}(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})[\mathbf{y}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]
the algebra of polynomials with coeffiecients in
H^{*}(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})
.
Then,

F_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}\colon H^{*}(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})[\mathbf{y}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]/\langle\mathbf{y}^{3}_{j}+(2-2g_{j})[\omega_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]\mathbf{y}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}\rangle\to H^{*}(\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}),\qquad F_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}(\beta)=\mathrm{pr}^{*}\beta|_{\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}},\quad F_{j}(\mathbf{y}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})=-\mathrm{e}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}},

is an algebra isomorphism.

As in Remark
5
, one shows

\mathrm{e}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}=\frac{1}{2}[\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]|_{\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}}

(22)

Similarly to Proposition
17
, one obtains a short exact sequence

0\to H^{*}(\mathcal{X})\to H^{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}})\to\oplus_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}H^{*}(\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})/H^{*}(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})\to 0,

(23)

where the first map is
\rho^{*}
, and the second is the composition of the
restriction
H^{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}})\to\oplus_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}H^{*}(\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})
and the projection to the quotient. Proposition
23
implies

H^{k}(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}})\cong H^{k}(\mathcal{X})\oplus\bigoplus_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}\left(\mathrm{pr}^{*}H^{k-2}(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})\wedge[\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]\oplus\mathrm{pr}^{*}H^{k-4}(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})\wedge[\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]^{2}\right).

(24)

We establish some identities for the product in
H^{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}})
, sketching the proofs only, as they are analogous to those in Proposition
19
.

Lemma 24
.

The following identities hold on
H^{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}})
:

\displaystyle[\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]^{3}=

\displaystyle 4\mathrm{Th}[L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]-(8-8g_{j})[\mathrm{pr}^{*}\omega_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}\wedge\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}],

(25)

\displaystyle\rho^{*}[\alpha]\wedge[\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]=

\displaystyle[\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\alpha|_{L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}})\wedge\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}].

(26)

Proof.

Using
F_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}(\mathbf{y}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})=-\mathrm{e}_{j,\bar{0}}=-\frac{1}{2}[\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]|_{\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}}
, Proposition
23
, and equation (
23
) we obtain

[\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}^{3}+(8-8g_{j})\mathrm{pr}^{*}\omega_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}\wedge\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]=[\rho^{*}(\gamma)]
, where
\gamma
is supported in
\rho(B_{\delta}(\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}))
. In addition, for
x\in L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}
we have:

\int_{\nu_{x}(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\gamma=\int_{(\mathcal{P}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})_{x}}{\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}^{3}}=\int_{(\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})_{x}}{\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}^{2}},

where we used that
\mathrm{pr}^{*}\omega_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}|_{P_{x}}=0
, and that
[\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]|_{P_{x}}
is the Poincaré dual to
(\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})_{x}
. In addition,
\frac{1}{2}[\upsilon_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]|_{(\mathcal{Q}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})_{x}}
is the Euler class of the bundle
(\mathcal{S}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})_{x}\to({\mathcal{Q}}_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})_{x}
. Identifying this with the tautological line bundle over
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, the Euler class is
-[\eta_{H}]
, where
\eta_{H}=\frac{i}{2\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}\log{r^{2}}
represents the class of a hyperplane. Hence,

\int_{\nu_{x}(L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\gamma=4\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}{\eta_{H}^{2}}=4,

and
[\gamma]=4\mathrm{Th}[L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}]
. This implies equation (
25
). Equation (
26
) follows by representing
[\alpha]
by a form
\alpha+d\beta
, where
\beta\in\Omega^{3}(\mathcal{M})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is supported in
\sqcup B_{2\delta}(Q_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})
, and satisfies
\rho^{*}(\alpha+d\beta)|_{B_{\delta}(Q_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}})}=\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\alpha|_{L_{j,\bar{\varepsilon}}})
. The existence of
\beta
follows from the Poincaré’s Lemma.
∎

We now compute the first Chern class of the normal bundle of
\widetilde{X}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
.

Lemma 25
.

c_{1}(\nu(\tilde{X}))=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\tau_{j}
.

Proof.

First,
c_{1}(\nu(\widetilde{X}))|_{\widetilde{X}-Q}=0
since
\widetilde{X}-Q\cong M/\iota-L
and
\nu(\tilde{X}-Q)
is isomorphic to
\left((M-L)\times\mathbb{C}\right)/(x,z)\sim(\iota(x),-z)
. This bundle is trivial, as it is homotopic to
M/\iota-L\times\mathbb{C}
via

E_{t}=\left((M-L)\times\mathbb{C}\right)/(x,z)\sim(\iota(x),e^{\pi it}z)),\quad t\in[0,1].

From
the short exact sequence of the pair
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{X}-Q)
it follows that
c_{1}(\nu(\widetilde{X}))\in H^{2}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{X}-Q)
. By
Lemma
21
,
c_{1}(\nu(\widetilde{X}))=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}[\tau_{j}]
, for some
\lambda_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
. It remains to show
\lambda_{j}=-1/2
for all
j
.

Let
x\in L_{j}
, diagram (
20
) shows that
